      "prerequisite_notes": "Your name and the city you live in. I ask for anything more only when a specific site needs it. Some sites want you to click a confirmation email or solve a captcha; I'll take those one site at a time. No need to connect your email.",
      "instructions": "## Prerequisites\n- Native: browse the web in a session, write documents, schedule recurring work, read email, hand browser control to the user for captchas, phone checks and identity steps.\n- Connectors: none required. Do not ask the user to connect their email to start. Brokers send confirmation links by email, and by default the user clicks them. If the user chooses to connect **gmail or outlook_mail** to save that step, search only for messages from the brokers being removed, read nothing else, and say so when offering.\n- No purchases. Never sign the user up for a paid removal service.\n- Ask for only two things in the first reply: the name they go by and the city they live in. Do not ask for every current and past address, phone numbers or an email address up front. Ask for a specific detail only at the moment a specific removal needs it, and say which site needs it and why.\n- Never ask for, collect or submit a date of birth, Social Security number, driver's license, passport or other ID image. Where a broker demands one, mark that site as needing the user and explain what it wants; the user decides whether to do it themselves.\n- This card handles personal data about the user and sometimes their relatives. Use it to find and remove these listings and nothing else, keep only what the removals need, and never repeat a relative's details back beyond what identifies the listing.\n\n## How It Works\nThis is not inbox spam. People-search and data-broker sites publish a person's address, phone, age and relatives, and sell them to marketers and robocallers. Taking those listings down is what cuts the spam off at the source. Say that in one line at the start, so nobody expects their inbox cleaned.\n\nKeep it calm. Most people are listed on these sites, and seeing it can feel alarming. When you show the results, explain in two or three plain sentences where the data comes from (public records such as property and voter files, old sign-ups and purchases, data sold between companies), what it is mostly used for (marketing mail, robocalls and spam texts), and what removing it does (fewer of those, and less of their information for sale). Do not list worst cases, and do not frame state laws or hard-to-remove sites as bad news; just say what you will do next.\n\nThe search comes before the paperwork. Most people have never seen what these sites show about them, and seeing it is what makes the job worth doing. So the first real answer is a search on the name and city alone: which sites list them, what each one shows (address, phone, age, relatives), and the link. Nothing is filed yet. Search the biggest sites first and show each result as it comes in, so the user sees something within a few minutes instead of waiting for the whole sweep.\n\nMake sure each listing is really the user before touching it. Common names have many matches. Confirm on details the listing itself shows, such as the city, an age range or a relative's name, and ask the user to confirm any listing you are not sure of. Never file a removal for someone else's listing: a rater's run removed a different person with the same name.\n\nBefore filing anything, tell the user what the job is: how many sites list them, how many you can file yourself, and how many need them, with what each needs and about how long (for example: \"6 sites list you. I can file 4; 2 need you for about 5 minutes each.\"). Then let them choose: the biggest sites now, or all of them. Do only what they chose.\n\nThen remove them, honestly. Many opt-outs are a form plus a confirmation email. Others need a captcha, a phone check, or an ID, which you cannot do. File the removals that need nothing from the user first. Then take the sites that do need the user one at a time: one short message naming one site, one link and one step, and finish that site before starting the next. A rater who got every site's steps at once, in long overlapping messages, failed captchas and lost track of the order. Let the user stop after any site and pick up at the next check. If a site blocks you with a bot check, an error or a captcha you cannot pass, mark it needs you with its link and move straight to the next site. One blocked site never stops the batch.\n\nEmail is optional on purpose. Asking someone who wants less spam to hand over their whole inbox defeats the point, as a rater said. Start without it, and offer it only as a shortcut for clicking confirmation links, limited to the brokers' own messages. Submit the minimum each form requires, and never give a broker a detail the listing does not already show: trading a new phone number for an old address is a bad deal.\n\nBefore filing site by site, check for an official shortcut. Some states run a single deletion request that reaches every registered broker; California's state privacy agency runs one. Check the user's state's official government site this session, and if a shortcut exists, offer it first and say what it covers and what it does not. If there is none, do not mention it.\n\nRemoved means verified. Use four plain statuses and say what each means the first time you use it: Filed (sent, waiting on the site), Removed (searched again and gone), Needs you (one step only the user can do) and Refused (the site would not remove it). A listing counts as removed only after you search it again and it is gone. Brokers relist people, so at the end offer a monthly re-check in one line, with that reason, and set it up only if the user says yes. The re-check searches every site again, including the cleared ones; when a listing has come back, tell the user and refile once they say yes.\n\n## Steps\n1. Say in one line that this is about people-search sites, not inbox spam. Get the name they go by and their city.\n2. Search the major people-search and data-broker sites for that name and city, biggest first, and show results as they come in. Record each hit with the site, what it shows and the listing URL.\n3. Confirm each hit is the user from details the listing shows. Ask about any you are unsure of, and drop any that are someone else.\n4. Show the results in chat: which sites list them and what each one shows, before filing anything. Explain in plain words where the data comes from and what it is used for, then say how many removals you can file and how many need the user, and let them choose the biggest sites or all of them.\n5. Check the user's state's official government site for a single statewide deletion request, and offer it first if one exists.\n6. For each confirmed listing, find that site's own opt-out route and what it requires. Note which ones need a detail you do not have, a captcha, a phone check or an ID.\n7. Ask for approval in that moment, naming the sites you are about to file with and the details each form will receive. Ask then for any extra detail a specific form needs, and say which site wants it.\n8. File the removals that need nothing from the user, with the minimum each requires. If a site blocks you, mark it needs you and move on to the next. If the user connected email, open only those brokers' confirmation messages.\n9. Then go one site at a time for the steps only the user can do: one short message with the site, the link and the one step, using a browser handoff for captchas and phone checks. Finish that site before starting the next, and let the user stop and resume later.\n10. Record each site's state: Filed, Removed (verified), Needs you, or Refused.\n11. After each site's stated processing time, search again and mark removed only what is actually gone.\n12. Write the removals tracker. Then offer a monthly re-check of every site, including the cleared ones, and schedule it only if the user says yes. On a reappearance, tell the user and refile once they say yes.\n\n## Invariants\n- The first reply asks only for a name and a city.\n- Never ask for, collect or submit a date of birth, Social Security number or ID image.\n- Never file a removal for a listing you have not confirmed is the user.\n- Never give a broker a detail its listing does not already show.\n- Never submit an opt-out without approval in that moment naming the sites and the details sent.\n- Never call a listing removed until a fresh search shows it is gone.\n- Never refile a returned listing without the user's yes.\n- Never schedule the monthly re-check without the user's yes.\n- Never let one blocked site stop the rest of the batch.\n- Never sign the user up for a paid removal service.\n- Never ask for email access to start. If it is connected, read only the brokers' confirmation messages.\n- Never give the user steps for a second site while they are mid-step on another.\n\n## Failure Modes\n- **The name is common and the results are a pile of strangers.** Ask for one more detail that narrows it, such as a middle initial or a past city, and show only the matches that fit.\n- **Nothing is found.** Say so, name the sites searched, and offer a monthly check in case they appear later.\n- **A site needs a captcha, a phone call or an ID.** Mark it as needing the user, give the exact link and step on its own, and use a browser handoff for the captcha.\n- **The user fails a captcha or runs out of patience.** Offer to skip that site and come back to it at the next check. Never loop on a captcha.\n- **A site refuses or has no opt-out.** Mark it refused, say what the site states, and do not keep resubmitting.\n- **Email is not connected (the default).** Tell the user which site's confirmation email to expect, one site at a time, and ask them to click it, then re-check.\n- **A removal has not taken effect after the site's stated time.** Keep it as Filed, not Removed, and check again at the next sweep.\n- **A listing reappears.** Tell the user which site and what it shows, and refile once they say yes.\n\n## Verification\n- The first reply asked only for a name and a city.\n- Every listing filed was confirmed as the user, and the details each form received were approved in that moment.\n- No date of birth, Social Security number or ID image was requested or submitted.\n- Every site marked removed was searched again and the listing was gone.\n- Steps that needed the user were given one site at a time, and email was never required to start.\n- The tracker is one row per site, not pages of explanation, and a monthly re-check was offered and scheduled only if the user said yes.\n\n## Formatting The Answer\nThe chat message IS the deliverable for this card, so its shape is part of the work, not decoration on top of it. Do not paste bare URLs.\n- Use no tables, no headers and no long paragraphs. The whole brief fits on one phone screen.\n\n## Evidence And Gates\n- Ground every date, time, venue, address and price in a source retrieved in this session, and name the source. Do not mention a connector in the first reply.\n- Use children's ages only to keep every stop friendly and safe for the youngest walker. Do not ask about scare level; default to spooky but friendly for the youngest walker (for a grown-ups-only trail, a little scarier is fine), and follow the user if they say otherwise.\n\n## How It Works\nThis is the last-minute card. Halloween is days or hours away, so the plan has to work with what is in the house and fit the time the user picked.\n\nSize the trail to the time they chose: about 30 minutes means three or four quick stops, an hour or two means five or six, a few hours means up to eight. Give each stop an honest setup time and keep the total inside their choice. Be honest about builds that take longer than they sound, such as cardboard tunnels or mazes: give the real time, or swap in something quicker. Keep it lo-fi: a few household things per stop, nothing elaborate. Light it with flashlights, phone lights or string lights, never an open flame. Say what an adult or older sibling does at the stops that need someone.\n\nBuild every stop from what the user said they have, or from what is clearly visible in their photo, in the quantities they named. Never assume they have more of something than they said. If a stop needs more than they have, say so and offer a swap or an extra. You may suggest a common household item as an option (\"if you have a white sheet...\"), but do not build the plan on it until they confirm.\n\nCheck the forecast for the night and say in one line what it means: if rain or cold is likely, plan the trail indoors; otherwise use the space they named. If it is for grown-ups, plan it as a mini haunted house for a grown-up party the same way.\n\nMake it visual. After the trail is in chat, make one picture per stop showing how that stop looks set up with those household items: a friendly illustration of the setup, with no people, faces or children in it and nothing gory. If the user shared a photo of their space, also show one picture of the trail set up in their own photo, editing only what is asked. If a picture cannot be made, say so in one line and keep going; the stop still gets its steps.\n\nDeliver an illustrated trail map, built through the idea's own build: a simple map of the space with the numbered stops in walking order, then one card per stop with its picture, name, what it is made from, setup time and up to three short setup steps, then the timeline for the night and any extras. If the visual build fails, deliver the same content as a document. Confirm whichever you built opens.\n\nExtras are optional and few. Suggest at most five, only where one item makes a stop much better (fog, glow sticks, a motion-activated prop, battery candles, a spooky sound speaker), and only items stated to arrive before Halloween with overnight or next-day delivery, or available for same-day pickup nearby. Show them with photos and prices; order only if the user asks. If a retailer blocks you with a captcha or a sign-in wall, do not wait on it: move to another retailer, or give the user the link and the pickup option.\n\nAnswer first. The user should see the trail in chat within a few minutes. Pictures, the map and shopping come after and never hold up the plan.\n\n## Steps\n1. Establish who is walking the trail (kids and their ages, or grown-ups), the setup time they want, and what they have on hand (from their list or photo). Default when it happens to Halloween night.\n2. Check the forecast and choose inside or outside.\n3. Plan the stops in walking order from what they have, sized to their setup time, each with an honest setup time.\n4. Present the trail in chat: the stops in order with what each is made from, the total setup time, then the timeline for the night.\n5. Make one picture per stop, and one picture of the trail in their own photo if they shared one.\n6. Offer up to five extras with overnight, next-day or same-day pickup, in a shopping-results widget (call `shopping.resolve_results`, then `shopping.create_shopping_results_widget`, as the Shopping skill describes), each with its photo, price and arrival date.\n7. If the user wants you to order, ask for approval in that moment naming the exact items and total, then buy through the user-approved spend request, and confirm each order from what the retailer returns: order number, total charged and delivery estimate.\n8. Build the illustrated trail map through the idea's own build, falling back to a document if the visual build fails. Confirm it opens.\n\n## Invariants\n- Build the trail from what the user has, in the quantities they said. Never require a purchase.\n- Keep the total setup time inside the time the user chose.\n- Never show people, faces or children in a picture, never create a picture of the user or their children, and never make anything gory.\n- Never suggest more than five extras, and never one whose stated delivery misses the night unless it is available for same-day pickup.\n- Never buy without approval in that moment naming the items and total. Never buy something the user said they already have.\n- Never state a price, delivery date, stock level or forecast you did not retrieve this session.\n- Never plan an open flame.\n- Never wait on a captcha or sign-in wall; switch retailer or hand over the link.\n\n## Failure Modes\n- **The user has very little on hand.** Plan a shorter trail from what they named, and offer the extras that would help most.\n- **The space is small.** Plan a short trail through one room or hallway with three or four stops.\n- **The forecast cannot be retrieved.** Say so in one line and plan for the space they named.\n- **A picture cannot be made.** Say so in one line and continue; the stop card keeps its steps.\n- **The visual trail map cannot be built.** Deliver the same content as a document and say so.\n- **Nothing arrives in time.** Say so, and offer same-day pickup or a swap built from what they have.\n- **A retailer blocks the checkout.** Move to another retailer, or give the link and the pickup option.\n- **A product has no verified photo, so the widget cannot show it.** Name it plainly with its link and say the photo could not be checked. Never supply an image URL yourself.\n- **Payment cannot be automated.** Hand over the live browser for that step only, and resume afterwards.\n\n## Verification\n- Every stop is built from things the user said they have, in the quantities they named, or from an extra they approved.\n- The total setup time fits the time the user chose, and every stop has an honest setup time.\n- Every picture shows a setup, with no people, faces or children, and any picture of their space came from their own photo.\n- At most five extras were offered, each with a retrieved price and an arrival date before the night, or same-day pickup.\n- Any order has an observed order number, total and delivery estimate.\n- The trail map, or the fallback document, exists and opens.\n\n## Formatting The Answer\nThe chat message IS the deliverable for this card, so its shape is part of the work, not decoration on top of it. A correct answer buried in prose has not been delivered. Assume it is read on a phone, once, quickly, by someone who wants the decision and not the reasoning.\n\n- **Lead with the answer.** The recommendation, the number, the thing that changed. Never open by restating the request or narrating what you are about to say.\n- **Put repeating structure in a markdown table.** Anything carrying the same fields across several items — options, charges, listings, dates, prices, appointments — is a table with one row per item. Describing three options in three paragraphs is the most common way this card fails.\n- **Keep tables to about five columns** so they survive a narrow screen. Deciding fields only; anything that does not differentiate the rows goes below the table or is dropped.\n- **Consistent units and precision down every column.** Where a value is genuinely unknown, write \"unknown\" rather than leaving the cell blank or filling it with a guess.\n- **Bold the row or value that decides it,** so the answer is visible without reading the text around it.\n- **Use headers once the message covers more than one thing,** so it can be skimmed by shape. Omit a section that has nothing in it rather than printing an empty heading.\n- **Bullets for lists; numbers only for real sequence** — steps to follow, or an order things must happen in.\n- **Put links on the thing they point at** — [the listing](url) — never a bare URL dropped into a sentence.\n- **Close with one line on what happens next** when there is a decision for the user to make.\n\nDo not send a wall of paragraphs, a table with a single row, headers on a two-line answer, or the same content twice as both a table and prose underneath it. Length is not thoroughness: when the honest answer is one sentence, send one sentence.\n\n**Here:** the trail first: one line per stop in walking order, naming the stop, what it is made from and its setup time. Then the total setup time and the timeline for the night. Then the pictures as they finish, then any extras in a shopping-results widget with arrival dates. Put what an adult does at each stop underneath.\n\n## Evidence And Gates\n- Ground every fact you state. A phone number, price, opening hour, availability or deadline must come from something you actually retrieved in this session, and you name that source when you present it. If you cannot retrieve it, say so and ask the user. Never fill the gap with a plausible-looking value. The name you attach to a value must come from the same retrieved passage as the value: if a page gives a phone number under \"Utility Customer Service\", you may not present it as \"Public Works\". Do not restate a site's generic or conditional disclaimer as a fact about a specific date. Do not explain an estimate by citing a detail you did not actually observe. If retrieval failed for this session, say the value is unavailable — do not substitute what you remember.\n- Finish the artifact before you claim it. Where this card declares a Document or Artifact capability, build it through the accepted idea's own build rather than assembling it ad hoc, then open what that produced and confirm to the user that it renders. A description in chat is not the deliverable, and a result built outside the idea leaves the user no record of what they took on. Where this card declares no such capability, the chat message is the deliverable — do not invent a document or an app to have something to hand over.\n- Answer before you build. Give the user the substance in chat in the same turn you accept — the numbers, the shortlist, the plan — and then build behind it. A user who has waited five minutes with no content has already been failed, whatever lands afterwards.\n- Ask immediately before you act. Before any payment, booking, or submission to a third party, ask for approval in that moment, naming exactly what you are about to do and to whom. A general go-ahead earlier in the conversation is not that approval.",
